# Release result audit (stream `rv-data`)

Branch `atlas-release-rv-data`. Every change made by this stream: file, JSON pointer, old -> new, reason, source URL and the verbatim quote that supports it. Rendered from the change logs written by the edit tool (`Editor.set/add/delete/log_only`); 898 logged edits plus 60 `verified_at` bumps (set to 2026-10-25 on every sourced object whose source was re-read that day; omitted from the tables).

Trigger: an independent reviewer found that a result record said more than its own verbatim quote (T2K's CP result: "CP-conserving values (0, ±π) excluded at 3σ" next to a quoted interval [-3.41, -0.03] that contains -π). The quote checker proves a quote exists in its source, but nothing compared the paraphrased fields (`name`, `key_value_text.value`, `significance`, `summary.plain`, `summary.technical`, `why_it_matters`) with the quote. `results/t2k-cp-violation-constraint-2020.json` (fixed by the integrator) is the model of the right wording.

## 1. What was done

1. **All 109 results audited** (every file in `data/atlas/results/`; 109 of 109 changed in at least one statement, date or significance). Every statement field was compared with the record's verbatim quotes and with the cached abstract/paper text of the cited source (`scripts/atlas_quote_check.py` cache). Fixed: numbers the source does not give (126 GeV is not "126 times a proton"; ATLAS ttH 5.8σ is 13 TeV alone, the 7+8+13 TeV combination is 6.3σ; ATLAS H→ττ 6.4σ is the 7+8+13 TeV combination, 13 TeV alone is 4.4σ; "twenty times the first run" for LZ WS2024 is about 4–5 times; "22 kg" COHERENT argon is 24 kg active mass; ...), "first/only/strongest/tightest/most precise/last/landmark/definitive/proof/confirmed" claims the source does not make (removed, or attributed to the page that makes them, e.g. "CERN states…"), significances and exclusions stated more broadly than the source (CL, model and scope added), interpretations the abstract does not give, and unsourced field-history claims.
2. **F13**: every `significance` is now a string with its unit (σ glued to the number, scope in words: observed/expected, local, which dataset; CL for confidence levels). The three bare floats (COHERENT 3.0 → "more than 3σ…", 3.9 → "3.9σ…", 6.7 → "6.7σ") and the five plain-string significances (K2K, T2K ×3, T2K CP) are now sourced objects with a quote.
3. **F14**: announcement dates recorded in `announced` (and `date`/`date_kind: announcement`) where a primary page gives an earlier public event: ATLAS Higgs 2012-07-04, ATLAS H→bb 2018-07-09 (ICHEP), ATLAS H→ττ 2018-06-08, ATLAS light-by-light 2019-03-17, DONUT 2000-07-21, KamLAND 2002-12-06, KM3NeT UHE 2025-02-12, JUNO 2025-11-19, IceCube press dates (Glashow 2021-03-10, galactic plane 2023-06-29, NGC 1068 2022-11-03, TXS 2018-07-12), Muon g-2 2021-04-07 / 2023-08-10 / 2025-06-03, NA62 2024-09, LUX 2016-07-21, SNO CC 2001-06-18, SNO NC 2002-04-20, UA1 W 1983-01-20 (seminar; 25 January press conference in the note), UA1 Z 1983-06-01. Where the earliest public date is the arXiv v1 line, `date` is that day (`date_kind: preprint`, A9) with the 'Submitted on' line as the quote; the journal year moved to `published`. The schema already had `announced`/`published`; no field was invented.
4. **F7**: Fermilab Main Injector, Recycler, Linac and Booster lifecycles rewritten with sourced dates (section 4).
5. **F19**: SURF depth (section 5).
6. **F11 (data part)**: PDF locators and operator claims (section 6).
7. **Independent review**: after the first pass four fresh agents re-read the 109 results and the facility/accelerator edits against the sources and reported (findings and what was done with each: section 7). They found no wrong number or invented significance in the 109 results; the findings were scope and wording omissions, dates whose quote did not carry the date, stale notes and word-limit breaches, all fixed except those listed in section 8.

Checks at the end of the stream: `python -m hepgraph.atlas.validate`: 0 errors; `scripts/atlas_quote_check.py` over `results/`: 417 verbatim, 42 segments, 4 skipped (INSPIRE-only records, never fetched), 0 missing, 0 near; `facilities/`: 109 verbatim, 5 segments, 1 skipped (CERN ROR geocoder), 0 missing/near; `accelerators/`: 158 verbatim, 6 segments; `experiments/dune`: 19 verbatim, 2 segments. The `static/atlas/generated/` bundles were not rebuilt or committed.

## 2. Conventions

* `significance.value`: "5.4σ observed (5.5σ expected), combination of Run 1 and Run 2 H → bb̄ searches"; "more than 5σ (ΔACP differs from zero)"; confidence levels stay "90% CL upper limit".
* A quote is either one verbatim passage or segments joined by "..." (each at least 12 characters, all present in the same source); the quote checker verifies every one.
* New source records are `src:rv-*` (`rv-` first pass, `rv-r2-` results 33–64, `rv-l-` lead). `rank` is derived and is left out of new records.
* Hand-formatted files (11 results, 7 facilities) were edited in place and not re-serialised.

## 3. Per-record notes (what each of the 109 results had, and what was checked)

### Results 1–32

Quote checker (`--only results/`): no missing/near/number-flag in R1 records (the 2 missing + 1 near seen are dune-protodune-sp-performance-2020 and juno-oscillation-parameters-2026, not R1).

New sources: src:rv-cern-press-release-higgs-2012-07-04 (home.cern, 4 July 2012), src:rv-atlas-briefing-hbb-observation-2018 (atlas.cern, 9 July 2018),
src:rv-atlas-briefing-htautau-observation-2018 (atlas.cern, 8 June 2018), src:rv-atlas-briefing-lbyl-observation-2019 (atlas.cern, 17 March 2019).

- atlas-higgs-observation-2012 — CHANGED: plain "126 times as much as a proton" (wrong: 126 GeV; proton ~0.94 GeV) removed; "completed the SM", "decade of precision" removed; F14 announced 2012-07-04 (CERN seminar); published 2012.
- atlas-htautau-observation-2018 — CHANGED: "confirming that Higgs couples", "first ... after b and top" removed; tech's 6.4σ was the 7+8+13 TeV combination (13 TeV alone 4.4σ); kvt backed by quote (confidence uncertain -> verified); F14 announced 2018-06-08 (ATLAS-CONF-2018-021 briefing).

#### F13
All significance strings of the slice now use σ glued, with scope (observed/expected, local, which dataset). Bare floats: none in R1 (coherent-* belong to R2).

#### F14 done
ATLAS Higgs 2012 (2012-07-04), ATLAS H->bb (2018-07-09), ATLAS H->tautau (2018-06-08), ATLAS LbyL (2019-03-17): announced + date + date_kind announcement; journal year in published. CMS H->tautau and CMS-LHCb Bs->mumu: date moved to the arXiv date (preprint), journal year to published.

#### Remaining / unresolved
- F14 not found on a primary page: Borexino CNO (Neutrino 2020 talk date unverified), Belle II B->Knunu (EPS-HEP/seminar dates not found), Belle X(3872) (Lepton-Photon 2003 talk not sourced), CMS ttH (preprint 8 Apr 2018; earlier public event not located), ATLAS squark/gluino (a CONF-note briefing exists, atlas.cern/updates/briefing/search-strong-supersymmetry, but the analysis differs from the final paper and the page date was not verifiable).
- No change needed: CMS H->bb (CERN seminar 28 Aug 2018 is after the 24 Aug preprint), ATLAS ttH (4 June 2018, after the 1 June preprint).
- atlas-higgs-observation-2012 `publication.doi` is the arXiv DOI (10.48550/arXiv.1207.7214), not the journal DOI (10.1016/j.physletb.2012.08.020 is on the arXiv page); left untouched (outside scope; it feeds the panel's "DOI (journal)" link).
- Stale "in this session" wording remains in a few untouched `note` strings (atlas-lbyl significance note etc.).

## 4. F7: Fermilab Main Injector, Recycler, Linac, Booster (`accelerators/`)


- fermilab-main-injector: old = one active interval, start "in operation by 2005; earliest 1968" (map drew it from 1970).
  new = approved 1991-10 (Fermilab dedication release: "Federal funding for the project was approved in October 1991") -> construction 1993-03-22 (Fermilab History and Archives: "The lab broke ground on the Main Injector on March 22, 1993."; the design handbook says project construction "initiated in June, 1992", kept in the note) -> commissioning 1998-09 (PAC 1999 paper: "The commissioning of the Main Injector (MI) ring began in September 1998.") -> active 1999-06-01 (release: "officially began its life ... with the dedication of the Main Injector"; history page "dedicated on June 1, 1999") -> existing 2024-07 shutdown and planned-run intervals unchanged. state_at: 1970 None, 1992 approved, 1995 construction, 1998 commissioning, 2000 active.
- fermilab-recycler: old = one active interval "proton stacking", start 2013 (earliest 1999) without a source tying it to 2013. new = commissioning 1999-05 (EPAC 2000: "Commissioning of this accelerator was started in May 1999."; first proton beam through a third of the ring 12 Jan 1999 in the note) -> active 2004-06-01 (Bhat & Spalding: "Commissioning the Recycler ... This milestone was met, on schedule, on June 1, 2004.") to 2011 (antiproton storage for Run II (2001-2011)) -> proton stacking from "late 2014" (arXiv:1705.03075: "just 6 batches in late 2014"), earliest 2011. 2011-2014 is left as 'possible' coverage: no source dates the conversion.
- Same defect found and fixed in fermilab-linac (operating start 1970-12-01, design energy 200 MeV) and fermilab-booster (commissioning 1970-12-21, active 1971-05-21 at design energy 8 GeV), both from the Fermilab History and Archives accelerator timeline.


Review round (commit b5ba8ef): Main Injector construction start now carries `earliest` 1992-06 (the Technical Design Handbook says construction "was initiated in June, 1992"; the 22 March 1993 groundbreaking stays the day), the active start is marked `approximate` (dedication of 1 June 1999, not first beam to the Tevatron), the Linac interval end (design energy 1970-12-01) cites the history page, the Recycler 2004-06-01 and 2011 bounds are marked `approximate` (commissioning milestone; end inferred from the Run II span), and the Recycler's open-ended interval since 2015 carries a note that no source says whether it ran after the Main Injector stopped in July 2024. `state_at` after the review: Main Injector 1970 none, 1992 approved, 1993 construction (possible), 1998 commissioning (possible), 2000 active; Recycler 1999 commissioning, 2004 active (possible), 2015 active.

## 7. Independent review: findings and disposition

Four fresh agents (no knowledge of the first pass) re-read results 1–37, 38–73, 74–109 and the facility/accelerator edits. Disposition of their findings:

* **Fixed**: CMS dilepton "full Run 2" (the paper uses 2016–2018) and the missing Randall–Sundrum scope; Belle X(3872) disagreement scope; ALICE J/ψ hedge ("appear significantly different"); date quotes that carried no date (Belle II B→Kνν, Belle X, CMS W, and 23 more) now carry the arXiv 'Submitted on' line with day precision; `announced` as sourced objects (CMS W, CMS Higgs, CDF, D0, SK 1998, DUNE far site); ATLAS Higgs "local" significance (body text) with the global 5.1σ; stale ALICE hypertriton "adjacent id" note; ATLAS squark simplified-model scope; LEP: model scope of the 98% statement and the 2005 combination removed from `key_value`; ORCA6 "degenerate branch" wording and the scope of "disfavoured" (the paper also says both orderings are compatible with the data); IceCube 2013 wording ("generic predictions for an additional component", deposited energies); LSND date (arXiv v1 2001-04-27); stale notes (ProtoDUNE date_kind, IceCube TXS collaborations, ICARUS beam, Daya Bay month); F13 strings that did not say what the number is (Daya Bay, Double Chooz, IceCube ×4, LHCb charm, MiniBooNE, SNO CC, NA62); JUNO and KM3NeT quotes that did not contain the stated values; MEG II, SK-Gd plain summaries over 60 words; UA2 Z "four of the eight events"; Muon g-2 2023 0.20 vs 0.21 ppm; MEG 2016 qualifier on SM backgrounds; MINOS sterile (no MINOS+ in the paper, model scope); NA62 2026 `published` (arXiv date, removed) and `announced` (CERN news item date); MiniBooNE 2018 comparison figures; MicroBooNE exception; T2K CP `date_kind` -> preprint; NOvA-T2K `date_kind` -> preprint; UA1 W date (seminar); sourced citation for the "3σ evidence" remark (T2K Nature paper); Linac interval end; Main Injector/Recycler status and bounds; SURF/DUNE depth sources; operator sources; four summary.plain over 60 words created by the edits.
* **UA1/UA2 INSPIRE-only records**: the four 1983 records rested on an INSPIRE API record that cannot be re-fetched (INSPIRE is not to be used). Facsimiles of the journal pages hosted on wikis.mit.edu (`src:rv-l-ua1-w-plb122-103-mit`, `…ua2-w-plb122-476-mit`, `…ua1-z-plb126-398-mit`, `…ua2-z-plb129-130-mit`; text layer read with pdftotext, OCR errors in the formulae, so quotes skip them) now verify their abstract quotes; titles and the INSPIRE-derived key values (UA2 Z mass, UA1 W 80 GeV with the facsimile as well) keep the INSPIRE source where the facsimile's OCR cannot carry the exact string.
* **Not changed**: see section 8.

Their quotes are classed `skipped` by the checker (4 quotes).
* **F14 announcement dates not found on a primary page**: Borexino CNO (Neutrino 2020 talk), Belle II B→Kνν (EPS-HEP/seminar), Belle X(3872) (Lepton-Photon 2003), CMS ttH, ATLAS squark/gluino (a conference-note briefing exists, its date could not be verified), Daya Bay (8 March 2012 seminar) and Double Chooz (Neutrino 2012), NOvA 2022 (probably Neutrino 2020), MicroBooNE, MEG II, SK DSNB 2025, SK solar 2024, NA62 2021, OPERA 2015, T2K 2014 7.3σ (probably EPS-HEP 2013), XENONnT B8, XENON1T (arXiv v1 17 June 2020 is the earliest sourced date). `lz-*`, `ua*`, `t2k-cp` already had dates.
* Same-year preprint versus publication-year: results whose arXiv v1 abstract page is not cached (homestake 1998, minerva 2023-02, kamiokande, borexino pp is day-precise from the Nature page) keep their publication date.
* `cms-higgs-boson-observation-2012` and `cms-w-boson-mass-measurement-2024` `published` ("2012", "2026") and the older `published` year strings of other records are bare strings, not sourced objects; their dates are right. `superkekb-luminosity-world-record-2026` has `date_kind: announcement` for an achievement date (the note says so); `announced` is null.
* `coherent-csi-cross-section-2022` has no `significance`: the paper's conclusion says "observing CEvNS at 11.6 σ", not added (context not checked).
* Operator claims whose quote does not say who operates (not fixed): amundsen-scott, argonne and psi (Wikipedia only), baikal, cern (name only), cjpl and mit (empty quote), kit-karlsruhe, km3net (bare string), mcmurdo-ldb, university-of-washington-cenpa, yale-wright-lab.

Locators give PDF page indices ("PDF p. N"), not printed pages, except where both are stated.
* Pre-existing and untouched: `sites/surf-4850-level-dune-far-detector.json /coordinates` quote (a PDF table row) is `missing` in the checker; `experiments/dune.json` beam-power display "1.0-1.2 MW" vs value 1.2 draws an A21 drift warning; `facilities/fermilab.json` has an A12 active-before-construction warning; the number flags of the RHIC, SLC and WIPP quotes.
* **Release step**: `static/atlas/generated/atlas.json` was last rebuilt before this stream and still carries the Main Injector as "in operation by 2005; earliest 1968". It needs a rebuild after merging; this branch did not touch `static/`.
* The independent reviewers were report-only and used the cached source texts; the `src:rv-*` pages (CERN, ATLAS, Fermilab, IHEP, KEK/JAEA, T2K, SNO, MIT facsimiles) were fetched on 2026-10-25.

| `results/cms-lhcb-bs-to-mumu-observation-2015.json` | `/summary/plain` | CMS and LHCb pooled their data to see, for the first time, a B meson decaying into two muons - a decay that happens only about three times in a billion. | CMS and LHCb combined their 2011–2012 data to make the first observation of a B_s meson decaying into two muons, a decay the Standard Model predicts to be very rare: about four in every billion B_s mesons produced. | 'happens only about three times in a billion' is not stated; the paper gives the Standard Model prediction of about four per billion B_s mesons produced | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1411.4413 — “The standard model predicts that the Bs0 → µ+ µ− and B 0 → µ+ µ− decays are very rare, with about four of the former occurring for every billion Bs0 mesons produced” |
| `results/cms-lhcb-bs-to-mumu-observation-2015.json` | `/summary/technical` | Joint CMS+LHCb analysis of 7 and 8 TeV data: first observation of B0s -> mu+mu- at >6 sigma, plus 3 sigma evidence for B0 -> mu+mu-; branching fractions compatible with the standard model. | Joint CMS+LHCb analysis of 7 TeV (2011) and 8 TeV (2012) data: first observation of B_s → μ⁺μ⁻ with a statistical significance exceeding six standard deviations, and evidence for B⁰ → μ⁺μ⁻ with a significance of three standard deviations; the branching fractions are statistically compatible with SM predictions. | numbers and wording aligned with the abstract ('three standard deviations' evidence for B0; 'statistically compatible with SM predictions'); 'sigma' removed | https://arxiv.org/abs/1411.4413 — “Furthermore, evidence for the $B^0\to\mu^+\mu^-$ decay is obtained with a statistical significance of three standard deviations. The branching fraction measurements are statistically compatible with SM predictions and impose stringent constraints on several theories beyond the SM.” |
| `results/cms-lhcb-bs-to-mumu-observation-2015.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The decay is strongly suppressed in the standard model, so its rate is one of the sharpest constraints on new heavy particles in flavour physics. | The paper says a difference between the observed branching fractions and the Standard Model predictions would provide a direction in which the Standard Model should be extended; the abstract says the measurements are statistically compatible with SM predictions and impose stringent constraints on several theories beyond the SM. | 'one of the sharpest constraints on new heavy particles in flavour physics' is not stated; replaced by the paper's and abstract's wording | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1411.4413 — “A difference in the observed branching fractions with respect to the predictions of the standard model would provide a direction in which the standard model should be extended.” |

| `results/cms-long-range-near-side-correlations-2010.json` | `/summary/plain` | In its first year CMS saw pairs of particles flying in the same direction but far apart along the beam, a pattern nobody expected in ordinary proton collisions and previously seen only in collisions of heavy nuclei. | CMS reported the first observation, in proton-proton or proton-antiproton collisions, of a long-range 'near-side' correlation: in high-multiplicity 7 TeV collisions, pairs of particles are emitted in nearly the same azimuthal direction while far apart in pseudorapidity (angle relative to the beam). | 'In its first year', 'a pattern nobody expected' and 'previously seen only in collisions of heavy nuclei' are not stated in the paper; replaced by the abstract's first-observation statement and its description of the feature | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1009.4122 — “This is the first observation of such a long-range, near-side feature in two-particle correlation functions in pp or p p-bar collisions.” |
| `results/cms-long-range-near-side-correlations-2010.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The ridge is a signature previously associated with the quark-gluon plasma; seeing it in proton-proton collisions raised the question of whether collective behaviour also arises in the smallest collision systems. | The paper notes that measurements at RHIC revealed that the long-range structure of two-particle angular correlations is significantly modified by the hot and dense matter formed in relativistic heavy-ion collisions, and that because the particle densities in the highest-multiplicity pp collisions at the LHC begin to approach those in collisions of relatively small nuclei such as copper, it is natural to search for n… | 'a signature previously associated with the quark-gluon plasma' and 'the smallest collision systems' are not stated; replaced by the paper's introduction | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1009.4122 — “Since the particle densities produced in the highest multiplicity pp collisions at LHC energies begin to approach those in high energy collisions of relatively small nuclei such as copper [10], it is natural to search for the possible emergence of new features in the two-particle correlation function from high multiplicity pp events” |
| `results/cms-top-quark-mass-measurement-2023.json` | `/name` | Top quark mass measured to 0.37 GeV | Measurement of the top quark mass: 171.77 ± 0.37 GeV | old name 'Top quark mass measured to 0.37 GeV' did not say that 0.37 GeV is the uncertainty | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.01967 — “The top quark mass is measured to be 171.77 $\pm$ 0.37 GeV.” |
| `results/cms-top-quark-mass-measurement-2023.json` | `/summary/plain` | CMS weighed the top quark, the heaviest known elementary particle, to a precision of about two parts in a thousand. | CMS measured the mass of the top quark, which its paper calls the most massive fundamental particle, as 171.77 ± 0.37 GeV from 13 TeV collision data; the abstract says the approach significantly improves the precision over previous measurements. | 'to a precision of about two parts in a thousand' was derived arithmetic not stated in the source; replaced by the abstract's own statement | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.01967 — “This approach significantly improves the precision over previous measurements.” |
| `results/cms-top-quark-mass-measurement-2023.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The top quark mass enters the electroweak fit and the stability of the Higgs potential, so every improvement in its precision sharpens tests of the standard model. | The paper says that, because of its large Yukawa coupling, the top quark dominates the higher-order corrections to the Higgs boson mass and a precise top-quark mass sets strong constraints on the stability of the electroweak vacuum; precise measurements can also test the internal consistency of the Standard Model. | 'enters the electroweak fit' is not in the paper; reworded to the introduction's statements | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.01967 — “Because of its large Yukawa coupling, the top quark dominates the higher-order corrections to the Higgs boson mass and a precise determination of mt sets strong constraints on the stability of the electroweak vacuum [3, 4].” |
| `results/cms-tth-observation-2018.json` | `/significance/value` | 5.2 standard deviations | 5.2σ observed (4.2σ expected) | F13: unit normalised to σ; the abstract's expected significance added | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1804.02610 — “The corresponding expected significance from the standard model for a Higgs boson mass of 125.09 GeV is 4.2 standard deviations.” |
| `results/cms-tth-observation-2018.json` | `/summary/plain` | CMS observed Higgs bosons produced together with a top quark and an anti-top quark, the most direct way to measure how strongly the Higgs couples to the heaviest known particle. | CMS observed Higgs bosons produced together with a top quark and an anti-top quark. The paper explains that on-shell top quarks are too heavy to appear in Higgs boson decays, so this production process is how the Higgs boson's coupling to the top quark can be constrained. | 'the most direct way to measure ... the heaviest known particle' is not stated; replaced by the paper's own explanation | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1804.02610 — “However, the top quark Yukawa coupling (yt ) cannot be similarly tested from the measurement of a decay rate since on-shell top quarks are too heavy to be produced in Higgs boson decay. Instead, constraints on yt can be obtained through the measurement of the pp → ttH production process.” |
| `results/cms-tth-observation-2018.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The top quark Yukawa coupling is the largest in the standard model and is sensitive to new physics; measuring it directly tests the Higgs mechanism where it matters most. | In the Standard Model the masses of elementary fermions are accounted for by Yukawa interactions, with a coupling proportional to the fermion mass. The paper says the top-quark Yukawa coupling cannot be tested from a decay rate, so constraints come from pp → ttH production; the combined best-fit signal strength normalised to the standard model prediction is 1.26 +0.31/−0.26. | 'the largest in the standard model ... directly tests the Higgs mechanism where it matters most' is not stated in the paper; replaced by the paper's own statements | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1804.02610 — “However, the top quark Yukawa coupling (yt ) cannot be similarly tested from the measurement of a decay rate since on-shell top quarks are too heavy to be produced in Higgs boson decay.” |
| `results/cms-w-boson-mass-measurement-2024.json` | `/summary/plain` | CMS measured the mass of the W boson more precisely than any other LHC experiment, and found it to agree with the standard model - unlike an earlier, conflicting result from the Tevatron. | CMS measured the mass of the W boson, which the collaboration calls the best-precision measurement of the W mass at the LHC, and found it in agreement with the Standard Model prediction. The paper notes that a measurement by the CDF collaboration at the Tevatron disagrees strongly with both the Standard Model expectation and the other measurements. | 'more precisely than any other LHC experiment' is now attributed to CMS's own headline; 'unlike an earlier, conflicting result' is reworded to the paper's statement about CDF | https://cms.cern/news/cms-delivers-best-precision-measurement-w-boson-mass-lhc — “CMS delivers the best-precision measurement of the W boson mass at the LHC” |
| `results/cms-w-boson-mass-measurement-2024.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The W and Z masses are related in the standard model, so a precise W mass is a sensitive probe of undiscovered heavy particles entering through quantum loops. | The abstract says the W and Z boson masses are uniquely related in the Standard Model and that a precise determination of their masses is important because quantum loops of heavy, undiscovered particles could modify this relationship. | 'a sensitive probe of undiscovered heavy particles' reworded to the abstract's exact claim (the masses' relationship 'could' be modified) | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.13872 — “A precise determination of their masses is important because quantum loops of heavy, undiscovered particles could modify this relationship.” |

| `results/icecube-evidence-extraterrestrial-neutrinos-2013.json` | `/summary/plain` | IceCube found 28 neutrinos far too energetic to have been made in Earth's atmosphere, the first solid sign that neutrinos reach us from far outside our solar system. | IceCube reported 28 high-energy neutrino events that interact inside the detector. | `results/icecube-sterile-neutrino-search-2020.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The strongest test of eV-scale sterile neutrinos through matter-enhanced oscillations in the Earth, constraining the interpretation of short-baseline anomalies. | A test of eV-scale sterile neutrinos through matter-enhanced oscillations of atmospheric neutrinos in the Earth, relevant to the sterile-neutrino interpretation of short-baseline anomalies. | `results/icecube-txs-0506-056-multimessenger-2018.json` | `/summary/plain` | A single very energetic neutrino arrived from the direction of a distant galaxy with a jet pointed at us, just as telescopes saw that galaxy flaring in gamma rays. | `results/icecube-txs-0506-056-multimessenger-2018.json` | `/why_it_matters` | First identification of a candidate astrophysical source of high-energy neutrinos and a founding result of multimessenger astronomy with neutrinos. | The abstract notes that individual astrophysical sources previously detected in neutrinos were limited to the Sun and the supernova 1987A, and that the origins of the diffuse flux of high-energy cosmic neutrinos remained unidentified; this neutrino-blazar coincidence "suggests that blazars may be a source of high-energy neutrinos". | old text said "First identification of a candidate astrophysical source of high-energy neutrinos and a founding result of multimessenger astronomy with neutrinos" (not stated in the paper; the abstract's own context sentence is now used) | https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.08816 — “Individual astrophysical sources previously detected in neutrinos are limited to the Sun and the supernova 1987A, whereas the origins of the diffuse flux of high-energy cosmic neutrinos remain unidentified.” |
| `results/icecube-txs-0506-056-prior-neutrino-flare-2018.json` | `/summary/plain` | Looking back through nine and a half years of data, IceCube found that the same blazar had already sent a burst of neutrinos in 2014–15, independently of the 2017 alert. | Searching 9.5 years of IceCube data after the 2017 alert, the collaboration found an excess of high-energy neutrino events at the position of the same blazar between September 2014 and March 2015, which it describes as 3.5σ evidence for neutrino emission from that direction, independent of and prior to the 2017 flare. | old text said the blazar "had already sent a burst of neutrinos in 2014–15"; the abstract reports "3.5σ evidence for neutrino emission from the direction of TXS 0506+056" when time-variable flux is allowed | https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.08794 — “Allowing for time-variable flux, this constitutes 3.5σ evidence for neutrino emission from the direction of TXS 0506+056, independent of and prior to the 2017 flaring episode.” |
| `results/icecube-txs-0506-056-prior-neutrino-flare-2018.json` | `/why_it_matters` | Independent, archival evidence that blazars are sources of high-energy astrophysical neutrinos. | The abstract says the 2014–2015 excess is independent of and prior to the 2017 flaring episode and that this suggests blazars are the first identifiable sources of the high-energy astrophysical neutrino flux. | old text said "Independent, archival evidence that blazars are sources of high-energy astrophysical neutrinos" (the abstract says the excess "suggests that blazars are the first identifiable sources") | https://arxiv.org/abs/1807.08794 — “This suggests that blazars are the first identifiable sources of the high-energy astrophysical neutrino flux.” |

| `results/km3net-uhe-neutrino-observation-2025.json` | `/summary/plain` | KM3NeT's ARCA telescope caught the most energetic neutrino ever recorded, a near-horizontal flash of light from a particle interaction on the sea floor in February 2023, suggesting an origin far more powerful than any neutrino source seen before. | KM3NeT's ARCA telescope in the Mediterranean recorded, on 13 February 2023, a near-horizontal muon with an estimated energy of 120 PeV, which the paper says most probably came from a neutrino of even higher energy (about 220 PeV in the collaboration's announcement). The paper says the event's energy is much larger than that of any neutrino detected so far. | old text said the neutrino was "the most energetic neutrino ever recorded ... suggesting an origin far more powerful than any neutrino source seen before"; the abstract says the energy "is much larger than that of any neutrino detected so far" and leaves the origin open (a different cosmic accelerator, or a cosmogenic neutrino) | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1038/s41586-024-08543-1&resultType=core&format=json — “However, the energy of this event is much larger than that of any neutrino detected so far.” |
| `results/km3net-uhe-neutrino-observation-2025.json` | `/summary/technical` | Single ultra-high-energy through-going muon event (KM3-230213A), recorded 2023-02-13 by 21 active KM3NeT/ARCA detection units, with reconstructed muon energy 120 PeV implying a still higher parent-neutrino energy (~220 PeV, Collaboration estimate); the highest-energy neutrino candidate reported to date, published in Nature 638, 376-382 (2025). | Single ultra-high-energy through-going muon event (KM3-230213A), recorded on 2023-02-13 by KM3NeT/ARCA with 21 detection lines installed at the time; estimated muon energy 120 (−60/+110) PeV, produced most probably by a neutrino of even higher energy (about 220 PeV in the collaboration's estimate). The paper says the energy of this event is much larger than that of any neutrino detected so far. Published in Nature 63… | old text called it "the highest-energy neutrino candidate reported to date" and wrote the muon energy as a bare 120 PeV; the abstract gives 120 (−60/+110) PeV and "much larger than that of any neutrino detected so far" | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1038/s41586-024-08543-1&resultType=core&format=json — “In light of its enormous energy and near-horizontal direction, the muon most probably originated from the interaction of a neutrino of even higher energy in the vicinity of the detector.” |
| `results/km3net-uhe-neutrino-observation-2025.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The event's energy far exceeds any previously detected neutrino, implying either a new class of cosmic accelerator or the first detection of a cosmogenic neutrino produced by ultra-high-energy cosmic rays interacting with the cosmic microwave background. | The abstract says the neutrino may have originated in a different cosmic accelerator than the lower-energy neutrinos, or may be the first detection of a cosmogenic neutrino produced by interactions of ultra-high-energy cosmic rays with background photons in the Universe. | old text said the energy "implies" a new class of accelerator or a cosmogenic neutrino; the abstract says it "suggests" these two possibilities | https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1038/s41586-024-08543-1&resultType=core&format=json — “This suggests that the neutrino may have originated in a different cosmic accelerator than the lower-energy neutrinos, or this may be the first detection of a cosmogenic neutrino8” |

| `results/k2k-oscillation-measurement-2006.json` | `/why_it_matters` | First confirmation of neutrino oscillation using an artificial, well-characterised accelerator beam rather than natural atmospheric or solar neutrinos, validating the interpretation of the atmospheric-neutrino anomaly and establishing the long-baseline accelerator method used ever since (K2K, MINOS, T2K, NOvA, DUNE). | The paper says the K2K collaboration had previously reported evidence of neutrino oscillations in a man-made neutrino beam directed 250 km across Japan; this paper presents updated ν_μ disappearance measurements: 112 observed events against 158.1 expected without oscillation, a distorted energy spectrum, and an allowed Δm² region of 1.9 to 3.5 × 10⁻³ eV² at 90% CL. | old text said "First confirmation of neutrino oscillation using an artificial, well-characterised accelerator beam ... establishing the long-baseline accelerator method used ever since (K2K, MINOS, T2K, NOvA, DUNE)"; the paper itself says K2K had previously reported evidence in this beam, and the lineage claims are not stated | https://arxiv.org/pdf/hep-ex/0606032 — “collaboration has previously reported evidence of neutrino oscillations in a man-made neutrino beam which was directed 250 km across Japan” |

### Results 65–96 (lhcb-cp-violation-charm … sno-cc) (169 logged edits; 16 further `verified_at` bumps omitted)

| file | pointer | old | new | reason | source URL and verbatim quote |

| `results/lhcb-cp-violation-charm-observation-2019.json` | `/summary/plain` | For the first time, LHCb caught charm quarks behaving slightly differently from their antimatter counterparts, completing a set of matter-antimatter asymmetry observations across all quark families that carry this kind of charge. | LHCb found that charm particles and their antimatter partners decay in measurably different ways: the first observation of this matter-antimatter difference (CP violation) in the decay of charm hadrons. | `results/lhcb-cp-violation-charm-observation-2019.json` | `/summary/technical` | Measurement of the CP-asymmetry difference ΔA_CP between D0->K-K+ and D0->pi-pi+ decays using 6 fb-1 of 13 TeV pp data; combined with previous LHCb results, ΔA_CP = (-15.4±2.9)x10^-4, differing from zero by more than five standard deviations - the first observation of CP violation in the charm sector. | Measurement of the CP-asymmetry difference ΔA_CP between D0→K−K+ and D0→π−π+ decays using about 6 fb−1 of 13 TeV pp data; combined with previous LHCb results, ΔA_CP = (−15.4±2.9)×10−4, which the paper says differs from zero by more than five standard deviations. Three independent electron-neutrino searches all found rates consistent with the nominal expectation, and no excess of electron-neutrino events. | removed 'built to check ... mysterious excess' (own voice) and 'nothing unusual ... every time'; restated what the abstract and introduction say | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2110.14054 — “significantly improved ability to distinguish whether an electromagnetic shower is electron or photon-induced compared to Cherenkov-based detectors such as MiniBooNE” |
| `results/microboone-no-low-energy-excess-2021.json` | `/summary/technical` | Using three independent electron-neutrino selections (exclusive two-body 1p, semi-inclusive pionless, fully inclusive), MicroBooNE finds no excess of electron neutrino interactions above the standard-model expectation from the Booster Neutrino Beam, disfavoring a simple electron-like-excess interpretation of the MiniBooNE anomaly. | Three independent νe searches in the Booster Neutrino Beam (an exclusive search for two-body CCQE scattering, a semi-inclusive search for pionless events and an inclusive search): results consistent with the nominal νe rate expectations and no excess of νe events observed. The paper says this picture appears to disfavor an interpretation of MiniBooNE's excess as arising purely from an anomalously high rate of charged… | 'disfavoring a simple electron-like-excess interpretation' restated with the paper's own scope, CL and caveat ('remains unexplained') | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2110.14054 — “contradicts the hypothesis that νe CC interactions are fully responsible for that excess (x = 1) at >97% CL” |
| `results/microboone-no-low-energy-excess-2021.json` | `/why_it_matters` | This was MicroBooNE's primary physics goal: using liquid-argon imaging to determine whether MiniBooNE's excess was made of electrons (as a sterile-neutrino oscillation would produce) or something else. Finding no excess in any of three independent, differently-systematic analyses disfavors simple electron-flavor explanations of the anomaly. | The paper says the multi-pronged search is aimed at investigating the as-yet unexplained low energy excess of electromagnetic activity observed by MiniBooNE. It concludes that its sensitive measurements are so far inconsistent with an electron-neutrino interpretation of the excess, while the excess itself remains unexplained. | removed 'This was MicroBooNE's primary physics goal' and 'disfavors simple electron-flavor explanations' (unscoped); replaced by the paper's own statements | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2110.14054 — “While the MiniBooNE excess remains unexplained, our sensitive measurements are so far inconsistent with a νe interpretation of the excess.” |
| `results/microboone-no-low-energy-excess-2021.json` | `/notes/0` | MicroBooNE was built specifically to test the MiniBooNE low-energy excess (result:miniboone-low-energy-excess-2021) using a technology that can distinguish electrons from photons; this 2021 multi-topology search found no corresponding excess. | This 2021 multi-topology search targets the MiniBooNE low-energy excess (result:miniboone-low-energy-excess-2021) and found no corresponding excess of electron-neutrino events; the paper states that the MiniBooNE excess remains unexplained. | removed 'MicroBooNE was built specifically to test the MiniBooNE low-energy excess' (not stated in the source) | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2110.14054 |
| `results/minerva-axial-form-factor-2023.json` | `/summary/plain` | MINERvA measured, for the first time with high precision, how an antineutrino bounces off a bare proton (rather than one bound inside a heavier nucleus), pinning down the proton's 'axial' size to 0.73 femtometres. | MINERvA reports what it describes as, to its knowledge, the first high-statistics measurement of an antineutrino scattering off a free proton (hydrogen) rather than a proton bound inside a heavier nucleus, and extracts the nucleon's axial charge radius: 0.73 ± 0.17 femtometres. | 'for the first time with high precision' lacked the abstract's qualifier 'to our knowledge' | https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1923034 — “Here we report the first high-statistics measurement, to our knowledge, of the ν¯μp → μ+n cross-section from the hydrogen atom” |
| `results/minerva-axial-form-factor-2023.json` | `/summary/technical` | First high-statistics measurement of the ν̄μp→μ⁺n cross-section on free (hydrogen) protons in the MINERvA plastic-scintillator target, avoiding the nuclear-correction uncertainties of deuterium-based extractions; yields the nucleon axial form factor F_A(Q^2) and axial charge radius r_A = 0.73 ± 0.17 fm. | To the authors' knowledge the first high-statistics measurement of the ν̄μp→μ⁺n cross-section on hydrogen, using the plastic-scintillator target of MINERvA; it extracts the nucleon axial form factor F_A(Q²) from free proton targets, without the nuclear corrections that neutrino-deuterium extractions require, and gives the nucleon axial charge radius r_A = 0.73 ± 0.17 fm. | added the abstract's 'to our knowledge' qualifier | https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1923034 — “Up to now, FA(Q2) has been extracted from the bound nucleons in neutrino–deuterium scattering, which requires uncertain nuclear corrections.” |
| `results/minerva-axial-form-factor-2023.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The axial form factor sets the normalization for quasielastic neutrino-nucleon scattering, a dominant interaction channel used by oscillation experiments (MINOS, NOvA, DUNE) to reconstruct neutrino energy; a free-proton measurement removes a major source of nuclear-model uncertainty from that calibration. | The abstract says the axial form factor had so far been extracted from neutrino-deuterium scattering, which requires uncertain nuclear corrections; the antineutrino-hydrogen scattering presented here can access it without the need for nuclear theory corrections and enables direct comparisons with lattice quantum chromodynamics computations. | removed 'dominant interaction channel used by oscillation experiments (MINOS, NOvA, DUNE) to reconstruct neutrino energy' (not in the source); replaced by the abstract's own statement | https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1923034 — “The antineutrino–hydrogen scattering presented here can access the axial form factor without the need for nuclear theory corrections” |
| `results/minerva-axial-form-factor-2023.json` | `/key_value_text/value` | Nucleon axial charge radius rA = 0.73 ± 0.17 fm, extracted from the first high-statistics ν̄μp→μ⁺n cross-section measurement on free (hydrogen) protons | Nucleon axial charge radius rA = 0.73 ± 0.17 fm, extracted from what the authors describe as, to their knowledge, the first high-statistics ν̄μp→μ⁺n cross-section measurement on free (hydrogen) protons | added the abstract's 'to our knowledge' qualifier to the 'first' claim | https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1923034 — “the first high-statistics measurement, to our knowledge, of the ν¯μp → μ+n cross-section from the hydrogen atom” |
| `results/miniboone-low-energy-excess-2021.json` | `/summary/plain` | MiniBooNE saw more low-energy, electron-like flashes of light in its detector than it expected from known physics: 638 more than predicted, a 4.8-out-of-a-possible-many-sigma excess. No one has agreed on why. | MiniBooNE reported a total excess of 638 electron-like events, at 4.8 standard deviations overall (the significance is limited by systematic uncertainties), from combined neutrino-mode and antineutrino-mode data. Its extra studies disfavor models of the excess based on photons entering or exiting the detector. | old text had a garbled '4.8-out-of-a-possible-many-sigma' phrase and 'No one has agreed on why' (unsourced); restated from the abstract | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2006.16883 — “The overall significance of the excess, 4.8σ, is limited by systematic uncertainties, assumed to be Gaussian, as the statistical significance of the excess is 12.2σ.” |
| `results/miniboone-low-energy-excess-2021.json` | `/summary/technical` | Combined neutrino- and antineutrino-mode MiniBooNE data (30.02e20 POT total: 18.75e20 neutrino mode + 11.27e20 antineutrino mode) show a 4.8sigma excess of 638.0+-132.8 low-energy electron-like events over the background-only prediction, updating the original 2018 result. The origin of the excess (sterile neutrinos, a misestimated background, or new physics) remains unresolved; MicroBooNE's 2021 search (result:microb… | Neutrino-mode data (18.75×10²⁰ protons on target, a 46% increase over previously published results) and antineutrino-mode data (11.27×10²⁰ protons on target) show a total excess of 638.0 ± 132.8 electron-like events; the overall significance of the excess is 4.8σ, limited by systematic uncertainties, while its statistical significance is 12.2σ. Beam-timing and radius-distribution studies lead the collaboration to con… | removed 'The origin ... remains unresolved; MicroBooNE's 2021 search found no corresponding excess' (not in this source; MicroBooNE has its own record); added the abstract's statistical-vs-overall significance | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2006.16883 — “We conclude that models of the event excess based on entering and exiting photons are disfavored.” |
| `results/miniboone-low-energy-excess-2021.json` | `/why_it_matters` | If it is not an underestimated background, the MiniBooNE excess would require physics beyond the three-neutrino Standard Model, such as a sterile neutrino or new interactions; it motivated the entire Fermilab Short-Baseline Neutrino programme (MicroBooNE, SBND, ICARUS) built to pin down its cause. | The paper's introduction says LSND and MiniBooNE reported excesses of νe and ν̄e charged-current quasielastic events in νμ beams, and that exotic models beyond the three-neutrino paradigm, for example 3+N oscillation models with N additional sterile neutrinos, have been invoked to explain these anomalies. | removed 'would require physics beyond the three-neutrino Standard Model' (conditional claim in own voice) and 'motivated the entire Fermilab Short-Baseline Neutrino programme' (unsourced); replaced by the paper's own introduction | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2006.16883 — “Exotic models beyond the threeneutrino paradigm that have been invoked to explain these anomalies include, for example, 3+N neutrino oscillation models involving three active neutrinos and N additional sterile neutrinos” |
| `results/minos-oscillation-measurement-2013.json` | `/summary/plain` | MINOS combined its own beam data with cosmic neutrinos passing through the whole Earth to make one of the most precise measurements yet of how strongly muon neutrinos change type. | MINOS combined its own accelerator-beam data with atmospheric neutrinos to measure how muon neutrinos and antineutrinos disappear on the way to the detector, and found the neutrino and antineutrino mass splittings in excellent agreement. The paper calls these the world's most precise measurements to date of the mass splittings for both. | 'one of the most precise ... how strongly muon neutrinos change type' replaced by the paper's own conclusion about the mass splittings | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1304.6335 — “These results provide the world’s most precise measurement to date of these mass splittings for both neutrinos and antineutrinos.” |
| `results/minos-oscillation-measurement-2013.json` | `/why_it_matters` | Comparing neutrino and antineutrino oscillation parameters from the same detector is a direct test of CPT symmetry and helped establish the precision baseline against which later experiments (MINOS+, NOvA, T2K) measure the same quantities. | The paper notes that MINOS can separate neutrinos and antineutrinos event by event and, coupled with the world's only set of long-baseline accelerator antineutrino data, allows a comparison of the neutrino and antineutrino mass splittings, which it finds in excellent agreement: \|Δm²\| − \|Δm̄²\| = (0.12 +0.24 −0.26)×10⁻³ eV². | removed 'a direct test of CPT symmetry' (CPT is not mentioned in the source) and 'helped establish the precision baseline against which later experiments (MINOS+, NOvA, T2K) measure' (unsourced) | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1304.6335 — “A comparison of the neutrino and antineutrino mass splittings shows them to be in excellent agreement.” |
| `results/minos-sterile-neutrino-limit-2016.json` | `/summary/plain` | MINOS looked for its muon neutrinos secretly turning into an undetectable 'sterile' neutrino and found no sign of it, setting the tightest limit at the time on how much that could be happening. | MINOS searched for muon neutrinos turning into undetectable 'sterile' neutrinos, over distances of 1.04 and 735 kilometres, and found no evidence of it. The paper sets what it calls a world-leading limit on the mixing angle θ24 for small mass splittings (Δm²41 up to about 1 eV²). | 'tightest limit at the time on how much that could be happening' replaced by the abstract's own wording and scope | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1607.01176 — “No evidence for νµ → νs transitions is found and we set a world-leading limit on θ24 for values of ∆m241 . 1 eV2 .” |
| `results/minos-sterile-neutrino-limit-2016.json` | `/why_it_matters` | Sterile neutrinos are a leading candidate explanation for the LSND/MiniBooNE anomalies; MINOS's world-leading limit on muon-neutrino disappearance into a sterile state constrained the parameter space available to explain those anomalies via nu_mu-nu_s mixing. | The paper's introduction lists the LSND and MiniBooNE excesses, the reactor anomaly and the gallium-source deficit as anomalous oscillation signals that may potentially be reconciled with the three-flavor picture by adding one or more sterile neutrinos; this search found no evidence for νμ→νs transitions. | 'Sterile neutrinos are a leading candidate explanation ... constrained the parameter space available' replaced by the paper's own introduction and result | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1607.01176 — “may potentially be reconciled with data supporting the threeflavor oscillation picture by the addition of one or more sterile neutrinos” |

| `results/muon-g-2-measurement-2021.json` | `/summary/plain` | Fermilab's first result: the most precise measurement yet of a subtle property of the muon, agreeing with an earlier Brookhaven measurement and together pointing to a possible gap in our understanding of particle physics. | Fermilab's first result for the muon's magnetic anomaly, measured to 0.46 parts per million. Combined with previous measurements of both μ⁺ and μ⁻, the new experimental average is a_μ(Exp) = 116,592,061(41)×10⁻¹¹ (0.35 ppm), which increases the tension between experiment and theory to 4.2 standard deviatio… | '4.2 sigma above the (2020 consensus) Standard Model prediction' replaced by the abstract's own wording (3.3 standard deviations for the Fermilab result alone; tension of 4.2 standard deviations for the experimental average); '(2020 consensus)' is not in the abstract | https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.03281 — “After combination with previous measurements of both μ+ and μ−, the new experimental average of aμ(Exp) = 116 592 061(41)×10−11 (0.35 ppm) increases the tension between experiment and theory to 4.2 standard deviations” |
| `results/muon-g-2-measurement-2021.json` | `/why_it_matters` | The muon's anomalous magnetic moment is one of the most precisely predicted and measured quantities in physics; a persistent discrepancy between measurement and Standard Model prediction would be evidence of undiscovered particles or forces. | The paper compares the measured muon anomaly with the Standard Model prediction recommended by the Muon g−2 Theory Initiative: the Fermilab result alone is 3.3 standard deviations above it, and the new experimental average increases the tension between experiment and theory to 4.2 standard deviations. | removed 'a persistent discrepancy ... would be evidence of undiscovered particles or forces' and 'one of the most precisely predicted and measured quantities in physics' (not in the source) | https://arxiv.org/pdf/2104.03281 — “The Muon g − 2 Theory Initiative recommended value [13] for the standard model is also shown.” |
